\subsection{Augmentation}
% OUTLINE 5.2.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.2.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Already in prose: small or specialised feature sets (MACCS, 3D pharmacophores, antibody mutation features) handled apart from the budget split.
% - Update needed: the existing paragraph says extras are ranked by random forest importance and appended one at a time if they improve the internal score. That is out of date. Extras are now ranked by correlation with what the base model gets wrong, and a whole block size is chosen at once (below).
% What augmentation is for:
% - Two uses, same mechanism.
%   - Use 1: one representation already dominates a dataset. Keep it at full width, then add a few features from the others.
%   - Use 2: small or specialised feature sets that cannot compete fairly inside the budget split (too few features, or only meaningful alongside something else). They are offered as extras after the main hybrid is built.
% - Terms: "base" = the feature matrix being improved (one full representation, or a finished hybrid $\mathbf{H}$). "Donors" = the feature sets the extras are drawn from.
% - Why it matters: a chemist who already trusts one representation (e.g. an ECFP model in use) can keep it. Augmentation reports which few outside features help, and how many.
% - Why it matters: this is a lower barrier to adoption than replacing the representation outright.
% - Links to: Chapter 4, "Different representations carry different information". Augmentation tests that claim directly: does another representation hold signal the dominant one lacks?
% - Links to: "Blueprints". The kept extras are stored in the blueprint with their source representation, so they can be read chemically where the donor allows it (e.g. MACCS keys).
% Which feature sets are offered as extras (set per dataset, not per domain):
% - Small molecules: MACCS structural keys (166 keys) \citep{durant2002maccs}, 3D pharmacophore features, USR shape descriptors \citep{ballester2007usr}, USRCAT (USR plus pharmacophore channels) \citep{schreyer2012usrcat}, Coulomb matrix eigenvalues \citep{rupp2012coulomb}.
%   - MACCS is a donor on most small-molecule datasets but sits in the main pool on one. Word this as "usually a donor".
%   - On a few datasets a learned embedding or torsion fingerprint is a donor instead of a main representation.
% - Antibodies: mutation features (differences from a parent sequence), protein language model log-likelihood ratios (ESM-2, AntiBERTy), amino acid composition, paratope features, structural features.
% - Proteins (drug-target sets): pocket descriptors, protein shape descriptors, residue contact-graph features, amino acid composition.
% - Nucleotides: codon usage, GC content, dinucleotide frequencies, DNA shape, sequence complexity.
% - Some datasets have no donors, so they are not augmented.
% - Common thread: each donor is low-dimensional or describes one aspect only (shape, the mutated positions). Useful as a supplement, weak as a whole representation.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on combining 3D shape/pharmacophore descriptors with 2D fingerprints in QSAR models.
% How it works:
% - Step 1, internal split: the training fold is split once more, about 80% internal-train and 20% internal-validation. The split is group-aware (scaffold or sequence groups kept together). The outer test fold is never touched.
% - Step 2, what the base gets wrong: fit a small random forest (50 trees, depth 10) on the base features. Use 3-fold group-aware cross-validation inside internal-train. Take the out-of-fold residuals: $r_i = y_i - \hat{y}^{\mathrm{oof}}_i$.
%   - Out-of-fold, not in-sample: a forest's in-sample residuals shrink towards zero and mostly reflect fitting noise.
%   - For binary classification, $\hat{y}$ is the predicted probability of the positive class.
%   - The residual model is always this cheap forest, not the downstream model. This keeps the ranking cost fixed.
% - Step 3, rank donor features: pool every feature from every donor. Score each by $c_j = |\rho_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathbf{x}_j, \mathbf{r})|$ (Pearson correlation, on internal-train). Keep the top $M = 50$.
%   - Why residuals, not labels: ranking against $\mathbf{y}$ promotes features that repeat what the base already knows. Ranking against $\mathbf{r}$ promotes features that explain the base model's errors.
%   - Same idea as fitting each new stage to the current residual in gradient boosting \citep{friedman2001greedy}.
%   - Same idea as picking the variable most correlated with the current residual in least angle regression \citep{efron2004lars}.
%   - Other ranking rules exist but are not the default: forest importance, LightGBM gain, mutual information and DeepLIFT against the labels, and DeepLIFT against the residuals.
%   - Limitation: $|\rho_{\mathrm{P}}|$ sees only linear, one-feature-at-a-time relations.
%   - Limitation: it does not see redundancy among the extras themselves.
%   - Limitation: by default every donor feature is pooled. A wide donor (thousands of features) gets more chances in the ranking than a 40-feature engineered set. An optional per-donor cap exists.
% - Step 4, choose how many: try block sizes $k \in \{0, 5, 10, 20, 40\}$, capped at the candidates available. Block $k$ = the top $k$ ranked features.
%   - Each block size is scored on internal-validation with the downstream model: score $S(k)$.
%   - Pick $k^* = \arg\max_k S(k)$, subject to $S(k) > S(0) + \delta$. Margin $\delta = 0$ by default.
%   - $k = 0$ (keep nothing) is scored first and wins ties. "No extras" is the default outcome, not a failure.
%   - Five whole-block choices replace about 50 one-by-one accept/reject decisions.
%   - Choosing among many options on a small validation set overfits the choice \citep{cawley2010overfitting}. Fewer choices leaves less room for this.
%   - Variant: instead of the fixed list, $k$ can be set as a fraction of the width: $k = \mathrm{round}(f \cdot B)$ when capped, $k = \mathrm{round}(f \cdot |\mathbf{H}|)$ when uncapped. That $k$ is taken without any validation comparison, and the margin is not used.
% - Step 5, rebuild: the chosen block (donor name and feature indices) is stored. It is re-extracted from the full data for the outer fold, like the rest of the hybrid.
% Two ways to fit extras against the budget $B$:
% - Uncapped: extras are appended outside the budget. Final width $= |\mathbf{H}| + k^*$, up to $B + 40$.
% - Capped: extras must displace base features. Base features are ranked once by forest importance.
%   - The weakest $k^*$ base features are dropped. Final width $= (B - k^*) + k^* = B$.
%   - Any unspent budget is filled before any base feature is dropped.
%   - Only in capped mode must an extra feature beat a base feature to earn its slot.
% - Why it matters: uncapped and no-augmentation at the same nominal $B$ are not the same width. Comparing them mixes "better features" with "more features". Capped removes the width difference.
% - Dominant-representation use: the base is one representation and $B = d_k$ (its full width). Uncapped is the natural mode there. Capped would drop the base's own features.
% - In the main search, a dataset with two or more main representations and a donor list runs three separate conditions: no augmentation, uncapped, capped.
% - The conditions depend only on whether the dataset has donors, not on how many main representations it has. The single-representation experiment (Complexity experiments) runs both capped and uncapped.
% - Augmentation is a separate condition, not a swept axis inside one search. Every other axis (model, budget, allocation, importance) is searched once per condition.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on adding a few selected descriptors from a second feature set to a primary molecular fingerprint, chosen by the primary model's errors (residual-driven feature addition in QSAR).
% - A decoy check tests whether the rule rejects useless extras; defined in "Complexity experiments".
% - TODO(results): how often $k^*=0$ is chosen per dataset and model, and the per-fold score of base alone vs base plus extras (uncapped and capped); numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): which donor feature sets are kept most often, per domain; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): how many decoy features are admitted in the decoy check; numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: flow diagram. Left: one base matrix plus several donor blocks. Middle: base forest, then out-of-fold residuals $\mathbf{r}$, then donor features ranked by $|\rho_{\mathrm{P}}(\mathbf{x}_j,\mathbf{r})|$, then top 50. Right: two branches. Uncapped appends $k^*$ features. Capped drops the $k^*$ weakest base features and adds $k^*$. Internal-validation score picks $k^* \in \{0,5,10,20,40\}$.
% - DIAGRAM (results, later): per dataset, a bar of the chosen $k^*$ per fold, coloured by donor source.
% Big picture:
% - Implication: if $k^*$ is usually small but not zero, one dominant representation is close to enough, and a few cheap extras fix specific gaps.
% - Implication: if $k^*$ is usually zero, the dominant representation already covers what the others offer.
% - Links to: "Simple is often better" (Chapter 7). Augmentation is the minimal version of the hybrid: one representation plus a few features.
% - Links to: "No single method fits every dataset" (Chapter 7). Which donors help, and how many features, is expected to vary by dataset. That is why $k$ is chosen per fold and not fixed.
% - Links to: "Beyond small molecules". Antibody mutation features and log-likelihood ratios reach the model through augmentation. For some antibody datasets this is how the domain-specific signal gets in.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.4 ----

% TODO: review, also I think this is out of date
Some features are too low-dimensional or too specialized to participate in the budget allocation stage: MACCS fingerprints (167 features), for instance, or log-likelihood ratios (3 features). Excluding them from the allocation would lose potentially useful signal; including them would distort the budget distribution because they cannot contribute many features. We handle these as augmentation: after the base hybrid is constructed, we pool the augmentation features, rank them by how strongly each correlates with the out-of-fold errors of a small random forest fitted on the base features, and keep the top 0, 5, 10, 20 or 40 of them, whichever scores best with the downstream model on the internal validation set.

% TODO: I need a better list to describe augmentations
Augmentation features range from smaller fingerprints like MACCS or Coloumb matrices, sets of structural information like 3D pharmacophores, and features describing only the differences between the sames like mutation features for antibodies.

% Already in prose: a short paragraph on adaptive pruning (allocation methods scored on the first fold, dropped if in the top 3 in fewer than 15% or 25% of groups). It is out of date and should be replaced: votes now use the first two folds, the allocation rule is best-of-2 at 20%, three more axes are voted on, there is a checkpoint at budget 100, and pruning is off unless a study switches it on.
% Two ways to make the search cheaper, answering different questions:
% - Fast tier: shorter lists on some axes, fixed before the search and the same for every dataset. Accepts a possibly worse answer for a much smaller grid.
% - Adaptive pruning: decided during the search from this dataset's own fold scores. Aims to find the same best answer while skipping configurations that have already clearly lost.
% - Why it matters: without these the search is impractical on large datasets. Links to: Chapter 7, speed as a stated limitation.
% Fast tier (checked in the code):
% - Downstream models: all five are kept. The engine's own fast list has only random forest, LightGBM and DNN, but the runs name XGBoost and SVM back so every experiment can read all five.
% - Allocation: linear, squared and rank decay. Softmax and the global pool are dropped.
% - Importance: LightGBM gain only.
% - Representation count: the top 3, or the whole pool.
% - Internal evaluators: unchanged (matched, random forest, LightGBM, DNN). Matched repeats one of them for random forest, LightGBM and DNN (3 evaluators each) and adds XGBoost or SVM for those two (4 each).
% - Size: given once, in Appendix "The search space and its defaults"; point there.
% - A fast run can be upgraded to a full one later without redoing work: each configuration's score is stored by its settings, not by the tier, so the full search reuses what the fast one scored and runs only the rest.
% - How the lists were chosen: a member stays in the fast lists if it is cheap and adds to the best score; an expensive member that wins often (e.g. the global pool) stays in the full search only.
% - TODO(results): the evidence behind the fast lists: which dropped members cost the most run time, and how much the best score moves when each is added back.
% - Which datasets use it: those where the full search cannot finish (large sample sizes, or run time beyond the cluster's job limits). Checked: twelve datasets run fast by default: nt_H3K27me3, nt_H3K9me3, nt_H3K9ac, nt_H3K4me3, gb_human_enhancers_cohn, nt_promoter_tata, qm9, herg, logd, flab_expression_koenig, tz_her2_binary, tz_her2_multiclass (tests/slurm_scripts/run_precompute_dataset.sh:104; same list in tests/experiments/dataset_policy.py:688-701). CHECK: the fast-tier bullet above says RF, LightGBM and DNN only; the precompute passes all five models at fast speed (run_precompute_dataset.sh:170, MODELS="rf,lightgbm,dnn,svm,xgboost"). TODO: mark them in the dataset table.
% - Dropped members are still searched on every other dataset. The fast tier is a compute compromise, not a claim that those members are useless.
% Adaptive pruning, in words:
% - After the first folds, some configurations are so far behind that scoring them on the remaining folds only confirms a loss. Stop scoring those.
% - Pruning is off unless a study switches it on. The main search computed once per dataset does prune, with the calibrated schedule below (as submitted, on every dataset; the per-dataset script alone would leave datasets under about 1,500 molecules unpruned). TODO: settle which, and say so.
% - TODO: state, study by study, which results used pruning.
% - CHECK: the per-dataset precompute does prune by default, contradicting "the main search computed once per dataset does not prune". Calibrated pruning on every dataset not in the SMALL list (the 12 fast datasets and all medium ones, e.g. caco2, buchwald_hartwig); no pruning on the 15 SMALL datasets (tcga_brca_pam50, flab_thermo_tresanco, humanness_therapeutic, herg_ki, five ginkgo sets, abcov_kd, three abagym sets, ddg_combined, cdr_flexibility). tests/slurm_scripts/run_precompute_dataset.sh:103-111.
% - Pruning is judged on one criterion: it must never delete a configuration that would have been the best. The cut-offs were set as those that lost no winner on a calibration set of datasets, not for the largest saving.
% Adaptive pruning, step by step (checked in the code):
% - Step 1: every configuration is scored on the first two folds. All rules below rank by the mean over these two folds. Budgets run from smallest to largest.
% - Step 2, checkpoint at budget 100: within each (downstream model, internal evaluator, representation count), rank every configuration at budgets up to 100 and keep the better half. An (allocation, importance) pair with no configuration in the better half is not searched at any larger budget for that group. At least one pair is kept.
% - Step 3, one vote per axis: groups fix every other axis, so values compete only against each other. A value is dropped from the remaining folds if it is among the best $t$ of its group in fewer than a cut-off share of groups.
%   - Allocation: best 2, 20%. Representation count: best 1, 10%. Importance: best 2, 20%. Internal evaluator: best 3, 10%.
%   - A vote runs only if the axis has more than $t$ values, and it can never delete every value.
%   - Configurations that failed to score are ranked last.
% - Step 4: survivors are scored on the remaining folds; the best is picked by the mean over all folds, as without pruning.
% Why these design choices:
% - Voting within matched groups: a value that is best for a random forest at a small budget should not be deleted for losing to a neural network at a large one.
% - Two voting folds, not one: on one small fold many configurations tie, ties are broken by list order, and a one-fold vote can delete good values by accident.
% - Pruning keeps the choice per dataset. The fast tier drops members on every fast-tier dataset.
% Caveat:
% - When pruning is on, the first two folds both choose the survivors and score them, so the mean over all folds is slightly optimistic \citep{Cawley2010}. Single representations are never pruned; a fair comparison under pruning leaves those two folds out or re-scores without them.
% DIAGRAM: funnel. Top: the full grid on folds 1-2. Next: the checkpoint at budget 100. Next: the votes per axis. Bottom: survivors on folds 3-5, then the best configuration. Annotate each stage with the share of configurations left.
% ---- END NOTES 5.2.5 ----

\subsubsection{Fast tier searching}

\subsubsection{Adaptive pruning}

Adaptive pruning: every configuration is first scored on the first two folds. At budget 100, within each (model, evaluator, representation count) group, (allocation, importance) pairs with no configuration in the better half are not searched at larger budgets. Each axis is then voted on within groups that fix every other axis: an allocation method is pruned if it is in the best 2 in fewer than 20\% of groups, a representation count if it is best in fewer than 10\%, an importance method if it is in the best 2 in fewer than 20\%, and an internal evaluator if it is in the best 3 in fewer than 10\%. Survivors are scored on the remaining folds.

\subsection{Datasets and splitting}
% OUTLINE 5.3.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section does:
% - Lists the datasets used in the hybrid experiments, grouped by domain: what is measured, number of samples, how folds are made, metric, source.
% - Why it matters: every hybrid comparison in this chapter is scored on held-out folds. How the folds are cut decides what "better" means.
% - Why it matters: a group split asks "does the model work on chemistry or sequences it has not seen?". A random split asks "can it recall near-copies?".
% - Links to: Representations by domain (base representations per dataset); Models and metrics; Appendix "Datasets and curation" (deduplication rules, repeat merging, counts before and after curation, sequence-identity clustering).

Columns: name and source; what is measured and its unit; task type (class balance if classification); number of samples after curation; metric and direction; what the folds are grouped by and number of groups; base representations. A line above the table saying one row is one dataset.

%
% Fixes needed in the existing prose (it disagrees with the code):
% - Prose says $R^2$ for reaction yield. The code scores both reaction datasets with RMSE on yield (%).
% - Prose says "5-fold GroupKFold throughout". Regression uses group k-fold.
% - Classification uses stratified group k-fold. Class proportions are balanced across folds, and groups are still never split.
% - The TODO in prose says reactions are grouped by template. The code groups them by additive.
% - The TODO says cancer data is grouped by patient. The code groups TCGA samples by tissue source site.
% - The TODO says antibodies are grouped by VH identity. The code clusters heavy + light chain at 95% identity.
% - The nucleotide datasets use MCC. The metric list in the prose does not mention MCC.
% - The prose metric list names "concordance index for drug-target affinity", and the prose TODO names SKEMPI and Ginkgo. None of these is among the 16 datasets. Drop them from the prose, or keep them only if those datasets come back.
% - CHECK: the prose cites Vieira2026. That key is not in refs.bib.
%
% General split rule:
% - 5 outer folds per dataset.
% - Every sample $i$ carries a group label $g_i$.
% - A group is never split: $\{g_i : i \in \mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{train}}\} \cap \{g_i : i \in \mathcal{D}^{(f)}_{\text{test}}\} = \emptyset$ for every fold $f$.
% - Regression: group k-fold.
% - Classification: stratified group k-fold. Each fold's class balance stays close to the full dataset's.
% - There is never a random-split fallback. With fewer than 5 groups, the search lowers the number of folds to the number of groups. With one group, the run stops with an error.
% - The grouping is chosen per domain to match what "unseen" means in that field (see below).
%
% Small molecules (ADME and toxicity; regression; RMSE; grouped by Bemis–Murcko scaffold):
% - Scaffold = the ring systems plus the linkers between them, with side chains removed \citep{Bemis1996}.
% - All molecules with the same scaffold go to the same fold.
% - hERG inhibition (Ki): 1,412 molecules; label pKi. Source: ChEMBL target CHEMBL240 \citep{Zdrazil2023}. Curation steps are in Appendix A.
%   - Why it matters: hERG block causes cardiac-safety failures in drug development.
%   - Experimental error in public Ki data sets a floor on RMSE; already in prose in this chapter's Limitations (Kramer2012). Point there.
% - Caco-2 efflux: 2,158 molecules; label log10 efflux ratio. Source: OpenADMET ExpansionRx data \citep{openadmet}.
% - LogD: 5,028 molecules; label LogD. Same source \citep{openadmet}.
% - Rat liver microsome clearance: 3,053 molecules; label log CLint. Biogen ADME set \citep{fang2023}, obtained through Polaris \citep{polaris}.
% - MDR1-MDCK efflux: 2,641 molecules; label log efflux ratio. Same source \citep{fang2023}.
% - Molecules per scaffold differ between datasets. hERG has about 1.8 molecules per scaffold (769 scaffolds). The Biogen sets are close to one molecule per scaffold (2,625 and 2,318 scaffolds).
% - So on the Biogen sets, a scaffold split is close to holding out single molecules.
%
% Quantum chemistry (regression; RMSE; scaffold groups):
% - QM9: HOMO–LUMO gap of small organic molecules, computed by DFT \citep{Ramakrishnan2014}.
% - The full set has about 134k molecules. 10,000 are drawn once with a fixed seed, to limit compute.
% - Folds are grouped by Murcko scaffold.
% - CHECK: 9,978 is quoted elsewhere as the count after curation (unverified).
% - The noise chapter uses ten replicate draws of QM9. This chapter uses one draw with 5 scaffold folds.
% - Why it matters: the label is computed, so it has no experimental noise. Any gap between hybrid and single representations reflects the representation, not label error.
%
% Reactions (regression; yield in %; RMSE; grouped by additive):
% - Buchwald–Hartwig C–N coupling, Doyle high-throughput set: 3,955 reactions \citep{Ahneman2018}. The loader documents 22 additive groups.
% - Second Buchwald–Hartwig set, taken from the Doyle lab's plate data table: 4,599 rows before curation. Rows with a missing aryl halide or yield are dropped.
% - The loader documents 23 additives for this set. The draft count of 24 groups may include a no-additive group (unverified).
% - Holding out whole additives follows the out-of-sample tests in the original paper \citep{Ahneman2018}.
% - With about 22–23 additive groups over 5 folds, each test fold holds 4–5 additives the model never saw in training.
% - The inputs are reactions, not molecules. Representations are computed on the reactants, the products, or their difference (see Representations by domain).
%
% Antibodies:
% - Koenig expression (deep mutational scan): variants of the anti-VEGF antibody G6; label expression enrichment; Spearman \citep{Koenig2017}. Taken from the FLAb benchmark \citep{Chungyoun2024}.
%   - Grouped by the position of the first mutation, mod 5, following ProteinGym \citep{Notin2023}.
%   - All mutations at one residue position stay in one fold. The model cannot learn a per-position effect and look it up at test time.
%   - Only 5 groups exist, so fold $f$ is the residue class $\{p : p \bmod 5 = f\}$.
% - Nanobody thermostability: 672 nanobodies; label melting temperature (°C); Spearman. From NbThermo \citep{ValdesTresanco2023}, via FLAb \citep{Chungyoun2024}.
%   - Grouped by 95% sequence-identity clusters (identity measure and clustering rule in Appendix A).
%   - Why: many entries are one or two residues apart, and grouping by exact sequence left these pairs on both sides of a split. 95% clustering keeps them together.
% - Humanness: 564 therapeutic antibodies; binary, human vs non-human; AUROC. From IMGT/mAb-DB via BioPhi \citep{Prihoda2022}. Grouped by 95% identity clusters.
% - Trastuzumab–HER2 binding: 5,000 CDRH3 variants, drawn from about 524,000 \citep{Chinery2024}. Binary, binder vs non-binder; AUROC.
%   - Grouped by edit-distance shell: the number of mutations away from the trastuzumab wild-type CDRH3.
%   - A held-out fold therefore holds variants at mutation distances not seen in training.
% - Why it matters: the antibody sets test whether the method works on sequence inputs, not only on molecular graphs. Links to: Beyond small molecules.
%
% Nucleotides (binary classification; MCC; grouped by chromosome):
% - Nucleotide Transformer benchmark tasks, revised release \citep{DallaTorre2025}.
%   - H3K4me3 histone-mark prediction: 18,244 sequences (dataset table count, unverified).
%   - TATA promoter detection: 5,274 sequences (dataset table count, unverified).
% - The benchmark's train and test files are merged, then regrouped by chromosome.
% - A whole chromosome, read from each sequence's genomic coordinate, is held out together.
%
% Cancer genomics (TCGA; classification; grouped by tissue source site; stratified group k-fold):
% - Lung adenocarcinoma vs lung squamous cell carcinoma: binary; AUROC. Data from TCGA \citep{TCGA2014LUAD, TCGA2012LUSC}, processed through the Toil pipeline \citep{Vivian2017}, downloaded from UCSC Xena \citep{Goldman2020}.
%   - CHECK: the dataset table says 1,016 samples; the draft said 1,017 loaded (unverified).
% - Breast cancer PAM50 subtype: 4 classes (Luminal A, Luminal B, Basal, HER2-enriched); weighted F1.
%   - Subtypes come from the PAM50 classifier \citep{Parker2009}. Data \citep{TCGA2012BRCA}, same processing \citep{Vivian2017, Goldman2020}.
% - Tissue source site = the institution that supplied the sample, read from the TCGA barcode.
% - The site codes are used only as fold groups (no batch correction; reason in Appendix A).
% - Why: grouping by site stops a model from scoring well by learning site-specific batch effects.
%
% Choices to state explicitly:
% - Group definitions differ by domain on purpose. The one shared rule is that no group spans train and test.
% - Some groupings leave few groups: 5 for Koenig, about 22–23 for reactions, one per chromosome for nucleotides.
% - Fewer groups make the per-fold scores vary more. Read spread per dataset, not across datasets.
% - Links to: the similarity chapter uses the same folds. Predicting each molecule's activity from its five nearest neighbours is scored on the same held-out groups.
% - Links to: Limitations. Scaffold splits can still leave close analogues across the split, because two scaffolds can differ by one atom.
% ---- END NOTES 5.3.1 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{5.3.1: Table 5.2, one row per dataset, grouped by domain.}

Different biological domains have established different conventions for what constitutes a good prediction, and applying a single metric everywhere would misrepresent performance in at least some of them. To this end, we assign each task a primary metric matched to its domain: RMSE for molecular property prediction, Spearman rank correlation for antibody regression and developability, AUROC for binary classification (including antibody classification and tumor subtype), weighted F1 for multiclass classification, concordance index for drug-target affinity, and $R^2$ for reaction yield.

No random splits are used. Random splitting inflates performance estimates for molecular data by placing structurally similar compounds on both sides of the train/test boundary \citep{Yang2019, Wallach2018}, and position-level leakage in deep mutational scanning data allows models to memorise site-specific effects rather than learning generalisable sequence-fitness relationships \citep{Notin2023, Vieira2026}. Splits therefore match domain conventions and use 5-fold GroupKFold throughout.

% TODO: [KB 183-191] gives the per-domain splitting protocols in full (Murcko scaffold groups;
% TODO: antibody inter-family by VH identity; DMS by mutation position mod 5 per ProteinGym;
% TODO: SKEMPI by PDB complex; Ginkgo pre-computed hierarchical clusters; reaction by template;
% TODO: cancer genomics by patient with subtype stratification). Carry them in as an itemized list,
% TODO: or defer to Appendix A. They are verbatim in KIRBy/PAPER_DRAFT.md.

The hybrid construction step uses a nested internal train/validation split within each training fold to score representations for feature allocation. This internal split is group-aware, taking the first fold of a grouped 5-fold split (GroupKFold) with the same grouping variable as the outer cross-validation, to prevent the allocation evaluator from seeing related samples on both sides of its split.


\subsection{Representations by domain}

% What this section is for:
% - Purpose: say which base representations enter the hybrid in each domain, what each family encodes, and why a pool mixes families.
% - Why it matters: the hybrid can only pick features that some base representation already carries. A pool of look-alike representations gives it nothing to choose between.
% - Links to: 4.2.5 (different representations carry different information) gives the motivation; 5.2.1 uses these as the $K$ matrices $\mathbf{X}^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d_k}$; 5.2.4 covers the augmentation features listed here.
% - Already in prose: any vector input can be a base representation.
% - Already in the tables of this section: what each representation encodes, its width, its source, and whether it is a base representation or augmentation. The bullets below give the motivation per family, not the per-representation detail.
% - Every representation is frozen: it is computed once from the sample (SMILES, sequence, reaction or patient profile) and never trained on the labels $\mathbf{y}$. Fine-tuned variants are handled separately and are not part of the pools below.
% - Links to: 5.1.5 (beyond small molecules). The same method runs on every domain; only the pool changes.

% Pre-processing (your TODO in the section):

This matters most for computed descriptors, whose scales differ widely (molecular weight in the hundreds, fragment counts 0 to 2). The same step is described in 5.2.1; keep one full description and point to it from the other.
% - Missing or non-finite descriptor values are set to 0. A molecule that cannot be parsed gets an all-zero row.
% - Checked (small molecules): the loaders DO call the standardisation routine for logd and caco2 (tests/find_best_hybrid_molecular.py:229) and herg_ki (:332). The Biogen/Polaris sets are only parsed by RDKit (:386). QM9 keeps the 129,428 RDKit-valid molecules (src/kirby/datasets/qm9.py:155-170). InChIKey deduplication runs on all of them (:542), skipped only for a set with one row per measurement. Full per-dataset steps are in the Appendix A notes. Reaction, antibody and Lhasa sets not checked here.
%
% How each pool of ten was chosen (short; method only):
% - Step 1: score every candidate representation on its own with a gradient-boosted tree model (LightGBM), under the dataset's real grouped folds. Drop the dataset if no representation clearly beats a trivial predictor.
% - Step 2: compare candidates by their out-of-fold predictions, not by their raw features: the Spearman correlation between two representations' prediction vectors.
% - Two representations whose predictions correlate at 0.95 or above are treated as carrying the same signal, so only one is kept.
% - Step 3: keep at most 10 that span distinct signal. The author made the final choice per dataset.
% - Some datasets have fewer than 10 because fewer distinct candidates passed: of the two nucleotide tasks, H3K4me3 has 8 and TATA promoter has 9.
% - Augmentation features are listed separately and do not count towards the ten. They are added after the hybrid is built (5.2.4). Most are small (3 to 167 features).
% - The same representation can be a base representation on one dataset and augmentation on another (MACCS keys, chromosome-arm expression summaries, mutation features, predicted-structure features). The tables must show this per dataset.
%
% How alike the base representations are:
% - Two representations of the same molecules can disagree about which molecules are close. If they disagree, each carries information the other lacks, or weights the same information differently.
% - Measure: compare the distances each representation gives, not the features themselves. Features of two representations cannot be matched one to one, but molecules can.
% - Step 1: standardise each feature.
% - Step 2: draw one fixed random set of up to 5,000 molecule pairs and reuse it for every representation.
% - Step 3: compute the Euclidean distance on each pair in each representation.
% - Step 4: agreement between two representations is the absolute Spearman correlation between their two lists of pair distances.
% - Near 1: both representations order pairs from near to far the same way. Near 0: one says nothing about the other.
% - It is a rank correlation, so a 2048-bit fingerprint and a 200-feature descriptor vector can be compared despite different distance ranges.
% - It needs no labels. It measures overlap of geometry, not of predictive content.
% - Caveat: low agreement shows difference, not usefulness. A representation can differ because it carries noise. Whether the differing part predicts the label is decided by the hybrid's importance scores.
% - A second measure, already computed when the pools were chosen: the correlation of each pair of representations' out-of-fold predictions (Step 2 above). The brain dump asks for these results here. What to report per domain, in words: on which datasets the strongest representations predict almost identically; on which datasets no pair comes close to identical; and whether the antibody variants that pool one language model over the CDRs only predict the same as the whole-sequence version.
% - Choosing each dataset's final set of representations looked at these correlations next to each representation's own score. The choice was made by hand.
% - TODO(results): agreement matrix for every representation pair on each dataset, with the median and the most and least alike pair; and the prediction-correlation summary above per dataset. Numbers come from the full runs on the final representation sets.
% - DIAGRAM: heatmap of agreement for one dataset per domain, representations ordered by family. Shows which families duplicate each other.
%
% Small molecules (input: a SMILES string per molecule):
% - Hashed substructure fingerprints: ECFP4 \citep{rogers2010}, atom pairs \citep{Carhart1985}, topological torsions \citep{Nilakantan1987}, Avalon \citep{Gedeck2006}. All 2048 bits.
%   - Atom pairs: every pair of atoms, described by their two atom types and the number of bonds between them.
%   - Topological torsions: every linear path of four bonded atoms.
%   - Avalon: a hashed mix of paths, rings and atom environments.
%   - What they encode: presence of local substructures. No 3D information and no global properties.
% - Predefined keys: MACCS, 166 fixed substructure questions (167 bits in RDKit) \citep{Durant2002}. Each bit has a fixed chemical meaning, so no collisions. Augmentation on most molecular datasets; a base representation on one.
% - Graph-kernel histogram: Weisfeiler-Lehman relabelling \citep{Shervashidze2011}, 5 rounds. Counts how often each atom-environment label appears.
%   - Close in spirit to ECFP, but counts are kept and nothing is folded. Labels absent from more than 95% of molecules are dropped.
% - Computed descriptors: 200 RDKit 2D descriptors \citep{rdkit} (connectivity indices, surface-area contributions, fragment counts, logP, weight), and Mordred, about 1,600 2D descriptors \citep{Moriwaki2018}.
%   - What they encode: whole-molecule physical and topological properties that a chemist can name.
% - Pretrained graph encoders: GROVER, a graph transformer pretrained on about 10 million unlabelled molecules \citep{Rong2020GROVER}; GraphMVP, pretrained to align 2D graphs with 3D conformers, only the 2D encoder used at inference \citep{Liu2022GraphMVP}; MHG-GNN \citep{kishimoto2023}.
% - Pretrained chemical language models, reading SMILES or SELFIES strings:
%   - MoLFormer \citep{ross2022}. The public checkpoint used was trained on 10% of the ZINC and PubChem molecules, not the full 1.1 billion.
%   - SMI-TED, an encoder-decoder model pretrained on about 91 million PubChem SMILES \citep{Soares2025}.
%   - ChemBERT: a community checkpoint (256 features, 8 layers) that follows the method of \citep{Zhang2022ChemBERT}, pretrained on ChEMBL version 33. It is not the authors' own released model; say so.
%   - ChemBERTa-2, the 77M-molecule multi-task checkpoint (384 features) \citep{Ahmad2022}.
%   - SELFormer, reads SELFIES \citep{Yuksel2023}.
%   - Each molecule vector is the mean of the last-layer token vectors, padding excluded.
%   - ChemBERT and ChemBERTa-2 are different models (256 and 384 features). Name them carefully in the table.
% - Mol2vec \citep{Jaeger2018}: defined in Chapter 2; point back.
% - 3D shape and pharmacophore features, augmentation only:
%   - USR: 12 moments of atom distances to 4 reference points \citep{Ballester2007}. USRCAT repeats this for 4 pharmacophore atom types, giving 60 features \citep{Schreyer2012}.
%   - 3D pharmacophore: counts of 7 feature types (donor, acceptor, aromatic, hydrophobe, positive, negative, lumped hydrophobe), plus mean and spread of the distance for each of the 21 type pairs, 49 features.
%   - Coulomb matrix eigenvalues \citep{Rupp2012}, sorted largest first, padded or cut to 50.
%   - All 3D features come from one conformer per molecule, embedded with a fixed seed and force-field optimised (MMFF). A single conformer is a limitation for flexible molecules.

% Antibodies (input: heavy and light chain amino-acid sequences):
% - Hand-built encodings:
%   - One-hot: each residue position as one of 20 letters, heavy and light chain padded to fixed lengths.
%   - BLOSUM62: same layout, each residue replaced by its row of the substitution matrix \citep{Henikoff1992}. Similar letters get similar vectors.
%   - Amino-acid composition: the fraction of each amino acid, per chain, 40 features.
% - General protein language models: ESM-2 650M \citep{Lin2023}; ESM3 \citep{Hayes2025}; ESM C 600M \citep{Candido2026}; ProtT5 \citep{Elnaggar2022}; SaProt, which adds a structure letter per residue \citep{Su2023SaProt}.
%   - SaProt's structure letters are Foldseek 3Di tokens \citep{vanKempen2024}, predicted from sequence by ProstT5 \citep{Heinzinger2024}, not taken from solved structures.
% - Antibody-specific language models: AntiBERTy \citep{Ruffolo2021AntiBERTy}; AbLang-2 \citep{Olsen2024}; IgT5 \citep{Kenlay2024IgT5}; Ab-RoBERTa \citep{Huh2025}.
%   - Why include both kinds: general models learned from all proteins; antibody models learned from antibody repertoires and see germline and CDR patterns more directly.
% - Most models embed heavy and light chains separately, and the two mean-pooled vectors are joined.
% - AbLang-2 and IgT5 read both chains together as one paired input. One ESM-2 variant joins the two sequences into one string and pools once (1280 features instead of 2560).
% - Pooling variants of one model, which count as separate base representations:
%   - Layer choice: mean over layers 0 to 16 ("early"), mean over layers 20 to 33 ("middle"), or layer 24 alone, instead of the last layer only.
%   - CDR only: mean over residues in the CDRs, found by IMGT numbering \citep{Lefranc2003} with ANARCI \citep{Dunbar2016ANARCI}. IMGT CDR positions: 27 to 38, 56 to 65, 105 to 117.
%   - Per-CDR: CDR1, CDR2 and CDR3 pooled separately, then joined.
%   - Most variable positions: the 20 positions per chain whose letters vary most across sequences (highest entropy), then the mean over those positions.

% - Mutation features, for datasets of point variants of one parent antibody (a base representation on some datasets, augmentation on others):

% Chemical reactions (input: reaction SMILES and, for high-throughput plates, the identity of each component):
% - Condensed graph of reaction: reactants and products merged into one graph; bonds that change are marked as dynamic \citep{Hoonakker2011}. Atom mapping by RXNMapper \citep{Schwaller2021rxnmapper}.
%   - Fingerprint: ECFP-style hashing (radius 2, 2048 bits) run on the condensed graph, with atom and bond labels that include the change, so changed bonds hash differently. This is the author's own construction; say so.
% - Reaction language models: rxnfp, 256 features from a BERT model trained on reactions \citep{Schwaller2021rxnfp}; ReactionT5 \citep{Sagawa2023ReactionT5}.
% - Component identity: one-hot of which aryl halide, ligand, base and additive was used. Encodes the plate design, no chemistry.
% - Any molecular representation applied three ways: to the reactants, to the products, or as the difference between product and reactant features.
% - Why it matters: on plate data, component identity alone can predict yield well. The hybrid shows whether chemistry adds anything beyond "which reagents".
% - \citet{Chuang2018}: random-valued features matched the chemical descriptors of \citet{Ahneman2018} on the Buchwald-Hartwig plate. \citet{Schwaller2021a}, Table 1, R$^2$ on random 70/30 splits: one-hot 0.89, DFT descriptors 0.92, Yield-BERT 0.951. Averaged over the four out-of-sample splits by additive: one-hot 0.59, DFT 0.69, Yield-BERT 0.73. So one-hot comes close on random splits and falls behind when additives are held out.

No labels are used. Either state this as label-free pre-processing on the whole cohort, or move these steps inside each fold.
% - Why it matters: here each representation is a different measurement of the patient, not a different encoding of one input. Links to: 5.1.5 and 5.9.

MOGONET classifies TCGA BRCA PAM50 subtypes from mRNA expression, DNA methylation and miRNA, combining omics-specific learning with cross-omics correlation learning \citep{Wang2021}.

For every data set in this study, we pre-selected ten base representations that spanned across different domains of information and showed some positive results when used on their own for the given prediction task. 


\subsection{Models and metrics}
% OUTLINE 5.3.3: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.3 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope: the downstream models that score every hybrid and every single representation, and the metric each dataset is scored in. The internal evaluators belong to the search-space section and get one link here.
% - Possible restructure: each dataset's metric can sit in its row of the dataset table (Datasets and splitting), leaving this subsection as a short paragraph on the models.

% - The prose says seven standard models, including GP, kNN and MLP. The default set is now five: random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, SVM and DNN.
% - The MLP was cut and the DNN replaces it everywhere. Ridge, CatBoost, the 1D CNN and the MC-Dropout BNN are not part of the experiments. Drop them from the list.
% - The prose gives the GP cap as 6,000 samples. Its cap is 1,000 training samples. The GP is added as a sixth downstream model on every dataset under 1,000 molecules, except those whose skip table already drops it (tcga_brca_pam50, flab_thermo_tresanco, tcga_luad_vs_lusc, ddg_combined). Say this in the models paragraph and give it the RBF-plus-noise kernel it uses.
% - The prose says there is no per-dataset tuning. That is true by default.
%   - An optional per-fold tuning exists: random search, 20 draws, 3 inner folds, group-aware, on the outer training fold only.
%   - The DNN is never tuned this way. It relies on its own early stopping.
%   - If any experiment in Ch 5 uses tuning, say so in that experiment's methods.
% Downstream models (default set):
% - There are five downstream models. Together they cover bagged trees, boosted trees, a kernel method and a neural network.
%   - Random forest \citep{Breiman2001}: 100 trees, no depth limit, $\sqrt{d}$ features tried at each split, where $d$ is the number of features in the input.
%   - XGBoost \citep{Chen2016}: 100 boosted trees. Each tree sees a random half of the features (column subsample 0.5). Library defaults otherwise.
%   - LightGBM \citep{ke2017lightgbm}: 100 boosted trees. Library defaults otherwise.
%   - SVM \citep{Vapnik1995}: RBF kernel with library defaults. The classifier outputs probabilities, so AUROC can be computed.
%   - DNN: fully connected, three hidden layers of 256, 128 and 64 units, ReLU activations.
%     - Dropout 0.2 \citep{Srivastava2014}.
%     - Adam optimiser, learning rate $10^{-3}$ \citep{Kingma2015}, batch size 256.
%     - Trains for at most 200 epochs.
%     - Early stopping: it stops after 20 epochs with no improvement on a holdout of 20% of the training fold.
%     - The learning rate halves after 5 epochs with no improvement.
%     - The early-stopping holdout is group-disjoint, using the same grouping as the outer split, so no scaffold group sits on both sides.
%     - Regression targets are z-scored inside the fit, with the training fold's mean and spread.
% - Implementation: scikit-learn \citep{scikit-learn} for the random forest and the SVM. XGBoost and LightGBM come from their own libraries. The DNN is written in PyTorch.
%   - PyTorch: \citep{Paszke2019}.
% - Hyperparameters are the same on every dataset: already in prose (end of the models paragraph). Keep that sentence.
% - Features are standardised in each fold with training-fold statistics only (described in 5.2.1). Binary fingerprint features are left as 0/1 only when a single representation is scored on its own; in the hybrid every feature is standardised.
% - Size cap: the SVM is skipped above 10,000 training samples (5,000 under the augmentation that adds features on top of the budget $B$).
% - A skip table also drops some default models on some datasets. Examples: XGBoost on every nucleotide dataset under every setting; SVM on caco2, logd and the ADME sets under capped augmentation; random forest and SVM on herg and qm9 under capped augmentation; random forest on herg, qm9 and logd under uncapped augmentation; SVM on the five large FLAb sets under uncapped augmentation. TODO: list it in Appendix C or the dataset table.
% - k-nearest neighbours is not a default downstream model ($k = 5$ when run as one). It is the model in the importance-weighted distance experiment (Complexity experiments), which reads 5, 10 and 20 neighbours, and Chapter 7 "Simple is often better" leans on that result. State its settings once, here, since it enters the argument.
% - Why five models: the best set of features depends on the model that uses it.
%   - Why it matters: a hybrid that helped only one model family would be a property of that model, not a better input.
%   - Links to: "Why each axis is swept" (the downstream model is one of the swept axes) and Ch 7 "No single method fits every dataset".
% - Tree-based models remain state of the art on medium-sized tabular data of about 10,000 samples \citep{Grinsztajn2022}.
%   - A hybrid is a tabular feature matrix, so tree ensembles are the baseline to beat.
% - Links to: "The search space" for the internal evaluators. Those are the models the search uses to score each representation on its own, which sets the budget split. They are separate from the downstream models reported here.
% Metrics, one per dataset (definitions are in Chapter 2; no formulas here):
% - Each dataset is scored in the metric its field normally uses, so the numbers can be compared with published work on the same dataset.
%   - RMSE, lower is better: small-molecule regression (ADME, hERG $K_i$, logD, QM9) and reaction yields.
%   - Spearman $\rho$, higher is better: antibody regression (expression, thermostability). Why: these assays are used to rank candidates, and their scales differ between labs.
%   - AUROC: binary classification (TCGA LUAD vs LUSC, trastuzumab-HER2 binder vs non-binder, humanness).
%   - Support-weighted F1: multi-class classification (TCGA BRCA PAM50 subtypes).
%   - MCC: nucleotide classification. It is high only when all four cells of the confusion matrix are good, which matters on imbalanced classes \citep{Chicco2020}.
% - Metrics are never averaged across datasets or domains. Each result is read in its own dataset's metric.
% Reporting:
% - Every score is on an outer test fold the search never saw. Report the per-fold scores and their spread, paired by fold with the best single representation (protocol in 5.3 and 5.2.1).
% - No significance tests and no confidence intervals, within or across datasets. With 5 folds, a p-value or an interval would rest on too few values to mean much.
%   - Fold scores share training molecules, so they are correlated. No unbiased estimator of the variance of k-fold cross-validation exists, and estimates that ignore the overlap between training sets grossly underestimate it \citep{Bengio2004}.
% - A gap is only interpreted if it is larger than the spread across folds. State this rule once, here, and apply it in every results section.
% Compute:
% - TODO(results): hours per dataset for the full search, and the hardware used (CPU cores, memory, and a GPU where the DNN or the embeddings used one); numbers come from the full runs.
% - Links to: "Fast tier and adaptive pruning", for how compute is reduced without changing the default models.
% Big picture:
% - Why it matters: five models, with one field-standard metric per dataset, let the thesis ask whether a better input helps every learner, not just one.
% - Links to: the Ch 5 Discussion (does the hybrid overfit the search?) and Ch 7 "Simple is often better". Fixed models with library defaults are a deliberately plain setting.
%
% NOT NOW - possible later addition, not among the current 16 datasets:
% - Antibody $\Delta\Delta G$ benchmark sets (Graphinity) would use Pearson $r$, to match the benchmark paper. \citep{Hummer2025} reports Pearson's correlation (up to 0.87 on experimental $\Delta\Delta G$).
% - Drug-target affinity (Davis, KIBA, BindingDB $K_d$, Metz) would use the concordance index \citep{Pahikkala2015}.
% - Trastuzumab-HER2 multi-class would use support-weighted F1.
% - ChEMBL per-target QSAR sets (dopamine D2, factor Xa, SARS-CoV-2 main protease) would use MAE.
% - Two antibody binding sets with censored labels (most values at the assay's ceiling or floor) would be scored as binder vs non-binder with AUROC, because a rank correlation says little when most labels share one clamped value. Links to Chapter 6, where censoring is one of the noise types.
% ---- END NOTES 5.3.3 ----

For a fair comparison, the downstream model pool must cover a range of inductive biases, from methods that degrade in high dimensions (kNN, SVM, GP) to tree ensembles that tolerate irrelevant features. Eleven models are available: tree ensembles (random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost, NGBoost), kernel methods (SVM, Gaussian process), nearest-neighbor (kNN), and neural networks (three-layer DNN, MC-Dropout BNN for uncertainty quantification, and a multi-scale 1D CNN). Standard experiments use five of these: random forest, XGBoost, LightGBM, SVM and DNN, with a Gaussian process added on datasets under 1,000 molecules. We use the full set for model-matching and noise experiments, and skip models with cubic or higher scaling for large datasets: Gaussian processes above 1,000 samples, SVMs above 10,000. No per-dataset tuning is performed: all models use fixed hyperparameters throughout, so performance differences reflect the input, not the model configuration.



\subsection{Complexity experiments}
% OUTLINE 5.3.4: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.4 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Purpose of the section:
% - These experiments ask how the hybrid behaves as a learning method, not only whether it wins on a dataset.
% - Main questions: does the search overfit its own selection; does a larger search give a better hybrid; is fusing features better than fusing model predictions; is the hybrid better than standard feature reduction at the same number of features; does the model used inside the search need to match the final model; do importance scores make a better distance for nearest-neighbour prediction; do the selected features stay the same from fold to fold; how does the gain change with dataset size.
% - Why it matters: the hybrid chooses among many configurations (allocation method, importance method, internal evaluator, budget). A reader will ask whether the reported gain is real or the reward for searching hard. These experiments are the answer to that.
% - Links to: Methods (the hybrid search: budget, allocation, internal evaluator); Discussion (overfitting on noise against robustness from a variety of features); Limitations (compute cost, re-running the search per model).
% - All experiments use the scaffold-group folds from Datasets and splitting and the reporting rule in Models and metrics.
%
% Shared protocol: pay the same selection cost on both sides:
% - Problem: the hybrid's score is the best of many configurations, while each single representation is one score. The best of many cross-validated scores is biased upwards \citep{Varma2006}.
% - Remedy used in several experiments: leave-one-fold-out reselection.
%   - For held-out fold $f$, pick the hybrid configuration with the best mean score on the other folds, $c^*_{-f} = \arg\max_c \frac{1}{F-1}\sum_{g\neq f} s_{c,g}$.
%   - Pick the best single representation in the same way, $k^*_{-f}$.
%   - Report $\Delta_f = s_{c^*_{-f},f} - s_{k^*_{-f},f}$, signed so that $\Delta_f>0$ means the hybrid is better (flip the sign for RMSE).
%   - It is arithmetic on stored per-fold scores. No extra model fits.
% - Both sides pay the same cost for being a maximum.
% - Selection inside folds: importance scores and the feature selection are always recomputed on the training rows of each fold only.
% - Where a configuration was chosen by a search over all rows and only the feature selection is redone per fold, the score is not fully held out. Say so wherever it applies (flagged below).
% - Already in prose (Discussion): Ambroise and McLachlan's result on selection outside the folds \citep{Ambroise2002}.
%
% Does the search overfit? (budget against held-out score):
% - Nested cross-validation as defined in "From base representations to a hybrid" (Step 8).
% - For every budget $B$, the winning configuration at that budget is rebuilt on the outer training rows and scored on the outer held-out fold.
% - Selection bias at each budget = the score the inner search reported minus the held-out score, signed so that a positive value means the search promised more than the held-out fold delivered (both metric directions). This is the single definition; the Discussion points here.
% - Budgets: the standard grid plus 4,000 and 8,000 features, and budgets at 0.5 to 2 times the number of training molecules. For budgets beyond the training-set size, a near-unregularised linear model is added, the one model whose number of fitted weights equals the budget; it is not one of the study's downstream models.
% - The two numbers to read per model: the budget each outer fold's search picked, and the budget with the best held-out score. If the search picks far above the best held-out budget, the search overfits.
% - DIAGRAM: x = budget $B/n_{\text{train}}$ (log scale), y = held-out score; one line per model; second panel for the selection bias.
% - TODO(results): held-out score and selection bias against budget per model and dataset; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Does a larger search give a better hybrid?:
% - The same hybrid-versus-comparator comparison is run with a small search (few configurations) and a large search (many configurations), on the same datasets and folds.
% - Comparators: the ensemble of one model per representation, the best single representation, PCA to the hybrid's width, and feature reduction at the same width (the experiments below).
% - Read: whether the hybrid's lead over each comparator grows with the number of configurations searched.

For classification, pooling is on predicted probabilities.

\subsection{Applied settings: OPIG and federated}
% OUTLINE 5.3.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.3.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% What this section covers:
% - Two applied settings: a set of collaborative prediction tasks with the OPIG group, and a federated setting where molecules cannot be pooled.
% - Both are method sections. They say how each task was set up and scored, not what came out.
% - Purpose: proof of concept that the hybrid search can be used in different settings. Each task needs a short paragraph of background on what the task is and how it is used.
% - Requirement stated once for all of them: several numeric representations of the same objects, one supervised target, and a grouped split that keeps near-duplicates on one side.
% - Links to: Representations by domain, which lists the representations these tasks draw on; Datasets and splitting, for the grouped-split rules reused here.
%
% Reusing a recipe across datasets:
% - Question: can a recipe found by searching on a large dataset be reused on a small one, instead of paying for a new search.
% - Source dataset is lipophilicity (about 5,000 molecules); target is hERG binding affinity (about 1,400 molecules).
% - The representation pool is restricted to the six representations present for both datasets at the same width, so the two searches range over the same feature space.
% - A recipe is the tuple (importance method, allocation rule, budget $B$, internal evaluator). It is a set of decisions, not a set of features.
% - The selected feature sets $S_1,\dots,S_K$ are the output of applying a recipe to a particular dataset.
% - Four arms are run inside every held-out fold of the target:
%   - best single representation, re-chosen from the fold's training rows;
%   - hybrid searched inside the fold's training rows, which is the ceiling;
%   - recipe transferred from the source, with $S_k$ re-solved on the target's training rows;
%   - the source's own $S_k$ applied unchanged, so no target label is used at selection time.
% - Five scaffold-group folds, root-mean-square error, several downstream models.
% - The two transfer arms separate two things that are usually confused: whether the search decisions generalise, and whether the chosen features generalise.
% - Why it matters: the search is the expensive part of the method, and a recipe that transfers turns it into a one-off cost paid on whichever dataset is largest.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on transferring a fitted feature-selection mask between QSAR datasets without refitting on the target.
% - TODO(results): whether a transferred recipe closes the gap between the best single representation and a hybrid searched in place.
%
% Hybrids on antibody mutational scans:
% - Four antibody mutational scans with a single wild type, including the expression scan among the 16 datasets \citep{Koenig2017}. Nested cross-validation: the search runs on outer-training rows only; the hybrid and every single representation are scored on the untouched outer fold.
% - What to report: whether the hybrid beats the best single representation on these scans, and which representations its features come from. The code's own question, whether two representations of the change from the wild type earn a place in the pool, is secondary; one line at most.
% - TODO(results): hybrid against best single representation per scan; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% The federated setting: what it is:
% - Federated learning trains one model across data held by several parties without moving the data \citep{McMahan2017}.
% - It has been run at industrial scale across pharmaceutical companies on shared property-prediction targets \citep{Heyndrickx2024}.
% - The motivation in this field is not privacy in the abstract: compound structures and assay results are the asset, so pooling them is not on the table \citep{Hanser2023,Bassani2023}.
% - The collaborator's process is distillation-based rather than gradient-averaging: one teacher model per site, a shared public set of molecules, and one student trained on the teachers' combined labels \citep{Hanser2025}. This thesis follows that implementation.
% - Distillation here means training a model on another model's outputs rather than on measurements \citep{Hinton2015}.
%
% How the teachers are combined:
% - Each teacher's vote on a public molecule is weighted by how close that molecule is to the teacher's own data (its distance to the nearest molecules in a sample of that site's fingerprints), so a site speaks loudest about chemistry it has seen. The student is trained on the most confident combined labels, the same number from each class.
% - A second weighting gives every site one weight from its own cross-validated score instead of a per-molecule distance. Only predictions on shared public molecules cross a site boundary.
%
% Selecting features under federation:
% - The addition here is that feature selection, not only model fitting, happens at the sites.
% - Each site runs the hybrid search on its own molecules: features are chosen on 80% of its rows and the resulting set is scored on the other 20%, split by scaffold group so the score is not read off rows the features were chosen from.
% - Each site reports three things: feature indices, importance values, and one score. No molecule and no feature value leaves.
% - The coordinator splits the total budget $B$ across sites in proportion to their scores (at least one feature each). Other splitting rules, including an equal split as the null, are swept alongside it.
% - A feature another site has already taken is skipped, so the hybrid has $B$ distinct features rather than $B$ slots.
% - Why it matters: what crosses the network is a list of indices and importances, which is smaller and less invertible than a model's weights or a gradient.
% - Why it matters: the allocation question the rest of the chapter asks between representations is the same question asked between organisations, with the same machinery.
% - Links to: Feature allocation strategies, where the within-dataset version of the same split is defined.
% - Federated feature selection where each client runs a mutual-information selection on its own data and a server aggregates the results, with no raw data exchanged \citep{Cassara2022}.
%
% The hERG comparison:
% - hERG channel blockade is a cardiotoxicity liability screened early in drug discovery, and its public data is assembled from many labs \citep{Czodrowski2013}.
% - Three real sources act as sites: one public activity database and two separately curated sets from the collaborator, one of which is a conservative call pooled over four independent published datasets.
% - This is source heterogeneity that exists in the world, not sites made by clustering one dataset, which is how the process is usually simulated.
% - Molecules held by more than one source are deduplicated on the standard molecular identifier, keeping the public copy.
% - Binary target: blocker if $\mathrm{p}K_i \ge 6$, so the cut is $K_i \le 1\,\mu\mathrm{M}$.
% - Five scaffold-group folds over the pooled molecules.
% - The search is driven by AUROC; the reported number is MCC, which does not flatter an imbalanced split.
% - The comparison is built as a two-by-two: molecules pooled or kept at their sites, crossed with all features or a selected set.
%   - pooled molecules and all features is the unrealistic upper reference;
%   - pooled molecules and selected features isolates what the selection is worth;
%   - molecules at their sites and all features is federated training as usually done;
%   - molecules at their sites and selected features is the only arm where nothing but indices and predictions moves.
% - Reading the corners: selected minus all features, at fixed site structure, is what selection buys; pooled minus site-held, at fixed features, is what pooling buys.
% - Four further arms give context: the single best source picked on training rows, the two teacher-weighting schemes, a control that runs the same search on one fingerprint alone, and one teacher per representation.
% - The budget $B$ is swept, not fixed, so no comparison rests on one width.
% - Caution to state in the text: the all-features arm fits a 2,048-bit fingerprint while the selected arm fits features drawn from six representations, so those two arms differ in width and in source at once. The budget sweep is what makes the difference readable; a single width cannot separate the two.
% - Links to: the noise chapters, since two curators disagreeing on the same molecule is label noise with a known source rather than injected noise.
%
% Other federated experiments, one line each (brief; say what was tried and whether it had any effect):
% - Sites with different amounts of label noise: federated feature selection against the teacher ensembles and naive pooling, at several noise levels, plus a version where each site first removes the rows it flags as noisy.
% - Representations as sites: every site holds the same molecules but a different representation, and feature-level fusion is compared with pooling the teachers' predictions.
% - Sites holding different families of representations: each organisation holds its own set of representations of shared molecules.
% - Importance per chemical region: molecules are split into k-means clusters, and importance is computed separately in each cluster, to ask whether the best allocation changes across chemical space.
% - Antibody sources as sites: three antibody sources (a binding-energy-change set, a deep mutational scan of binding, and nanobody thermostability) as sites, with language-model clustering in place of fingerprint clustering. Two of its planned arms are skipped because the sites' labels are in incompatible units.

\section{Discussion}
% OUTLINE 5.9: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 5.9 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Section scope: discussion threads. (1) Does the search overfit, or does drawing on diverse features make the model more robust? (2) Datasets where the hybrid does not beat the best single representation. (3) What a larger search buys, and what it costs in fairness. (4) Why matching the internal evaluator to the final model seems not to matter.
% - Already in prose: selection bias from selecting before cross-validation (Ambroise and McLachlan), selection as a form of model selection (Cawley and Talbot), search degrees of freedom (Tibshirani), the budget as a limit on capacity, frozen features against fine-tuning (Kumar et al.), and the Nogueira stability index.
% - Citation keys used in the existing prose are NOT in refs.bib: Ambroise2002, Vabalas2019, Cawley2010, Tibshirani2015, Ng2004, Kumar2022, Nogueira2018. Verified bibtex for all seven is returned with these notes.
% - CHECK the existing prose against the sources:
%   - Kumar et al.: fine-tuning is on average 7% worse than linear probing out of distribution. The "10%" figure is their combined method (linear probe first, then fine-tune) against plain fine-tuning, not linear probing against fine-tuning.
%   - Ng2004 does not argue that the feasible set grows polynomially in the budget. Ng shows that with $L_1$ regularisation the number of training samples needed grows only with the log of the number of irrelevant features; with $L_2$ it grows at least linearly. Rewrite or drop that sentence.
%   - The Ambroise and McLachlan "11 out of 40" figure was not re-checked here; check it against the paper.
%
% The case that the search overfits:
% - Features are ranked by importance computed on the same training labels the model is then fitted to. Noise in those labels can steer which features are picked.
% - The search picks the best of many configurations. The best of many noisy scores is biased upwards, even when each score on its own is unbiased \citep{Cawley2010}. The measured version of this is the selection bias defined in Complexity experiments; point there.
% - Prior work in this field: Sort \& Slice, which uses no labels to choose fingerprint substructures, outperformed two supervised substructure-selection schemes \citep{sns}.
%   - Checked: the Sort \& Slice paper says the reasons are "not obvious" and that it is "conceivable" that using the training label to select substructures adds to the risk of overfitting. A hedged suggestion, not a measured finding \citep{sns}. Dablander's thesis not checked.
% - Small datasets are the most exposed. With few molecules, one fold's importance ranking rests on few labels.
%
% The case that it does not, or that the cost is outweighed:
% - The held-out test is nested cross-validation: the search runs on the outer training set only, and its choice is scored on an outer fold it never saw, next to the single representations on the same folds (protocol in 5.2.1).
% - Picking the best single representation out of $K$ is also a selection step with its own optimism. The hybrid compares far more configurations, so its selection cost is larger; nested cross-validation charges each side for its own.
% - The search does not range over arbitrary feature subsets. Each representation contributes its top $b_k$ features by importance, so the number of candidate hybrids is the size of the configuration grid, not the number of possible subsets.
% - Stability (Nogueira index) is already in prose. Caveat to add: correlated features can swap between folds while carrying the same information, so a low index does not by itself show overfitting.
% - Blueprint transfer is a second check. A recipe chosen on one dataset and reused on another keeps its score only if the selection was not fitted to noise. Links to: the Blueprints method and the OPIG results.
%
% Robustness from diverse features:
% - A hybrid draws its features from several representations, so the model does not depend on one representation's blind spots (hash collisions; molecules a representation cannot tell apart). Links to: Chapter 4, flaws in standard base representations.
% - The tradeoff: part of any chosen set of $B$ features may be picked on noise. The question is whether the gain from strong, varied features from different sources outweighs that. The held-out outer folds answer this, not the inner search.
% - Links to: Chapter 6, where the importance ranking is computed on noisy labels, so that chapter tests the overfitting worry directly. Links to: Chapter 7, "Simple is often better": the winning hybrids often use fewer features than the largest budget.
% - TODO(results): per dataset and budget, the selection bias; whether the held-out score peaks and falls once $B$ exceeds the number of training molecules; the stability index per dataset. Numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: x-axis budget $B$ on a log scale, vertical line at the number of training molecules. y-axis metric in the dataset's own units. Two lines: score the inner search reported, and held-out outer-fold score. One panel per dataset. Individual fold scores as points; no error bars.
%
% What a larger search buys:
% - Pattern in the preliminary runs, to be confirmed by the full runs: the hybrid's lead over the comparators grows with the number of configurations searched (Complexity experiments). The method is a search method, and its value depends on how much search it is given.
% - Say whether the gap survives leave-one-fold-out reselection (the caveat that the comparators get no search is stated in Complexity experiments).
% - TODO(results): the lead over each comparator at each search size, reselected; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Matching the internal evaluator to the final model:
% - The comparison of matched, default and exact-match evaluators (Complexity experiments) is not a failed test. It says that the model scoring each representation during the search need not be the model that is finally trained.
% - Open question for the author: why this holds. One possibility to discuss: if the features that carry signal are the same for every model family, any reasonable evaluator finds them. This sits against the claim in "Why each axis is swept" that different models prefer different features; say how the two fit together.
% - Implication for Limitations: one cheap evaluator may be enough, so the search need not be re-run per model for this reason.
% - TODO(results): each evaluator setting against matched, per model, at matched width; numbers come from the full runs.
%
% Where the hybrid does not help:
% - On some datasets the best single representation scores as well as the hybrid, or better. Expected when one representation already holds most of the signal: adding features from the others adds little.
% - A tie there is consistent with the search not inflating scores; a search that still reported a large gain there would be the warning sign of overfitting.
% - Practical signal: score each representation alone before searching. If one stands well clear of the others, expect little gain from the hybrid. Links to: Limitations (high upfront cost for an unknown gain) and Chapter 7, "No single method fits every dataset".
% - TODO(results): the datasets where the hybrid does not beat the best single representation, and which representation dominates on each; numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(lit): search for literature on when combining molecular fingerprints or descriptors gives no gain over the best single one.
% ---- END NOTES 5.9 ----

Ambroise and McLachlan \citep{Ambroise2002} showed the extreme case of feature selection overfitting: an SVM achieved near-zero cross-validated error on random labels when gene selection was performed on the full dataset before cross-validation, but when selection was nested inside each fold the median error was 11 out of 40, close to the 50\% expected by chance. Vabalas et al.\ \citep{Vabalas2019} showed that the bias introduced by pre-selection can exceed the bias from hyperparameter tuning, and Cawley and Talbot \citep{Cawley2010} argued that feature selection is itself a form of model selection whose complexity cost must be accounted for. KIRBy follows the standard remedy: we compute all importance scores within each cross-validation fold, on training data only. But the problem of ``search degrees of freedom'' \citep{Tibshirani2015} remains. Searching over many possible feature subsets adds complexity even when each individual selection is unbiased. The budget acts as a hard capacity constraint: selecting 100 features from a pool of 5,000 limits the space of possible selections far more tightly than $L_1$ regularization over the same pool, because the feasible set grows polynomially in the budget while the continuous parameter space of a regularized model is unbounded \citep{Ng2004}. Important here is a distinction: the features being selected are frozen embeddings, not parameters being fitted. Choosing which pre-existing knowledge to trust is, we think, a less expressive operation than learning new features, and Kumar et al.\ \citep{Kumar2022} showed that frozen embeddings fed through a linear probe can outperform full fine-tuning by up to 10\% in out-of-distribution accuracy precisely because fine-tuning distorts pretrained structure. And stability can be measured directly: the Nogueira index \citep{Nogueira2018} quantifies the consistency of selected feature sets between folds. If selection were overfit, feature sets would differ wildly from fold to fold.


\section{Limitations}

Every configuration is scored on every fold unless pruning is on (see "Fast tier and adaptive pruning"), and under nested cross-validation the whole grid runs again inside every outer fold. The number of configurations is given once, in Appendix "The search space and its defaults"; point there.
