% - Importance methods differ in cost. SHAP values and neural-network attributions (DeepLIFT) cost far more than tree gain importance.
% - Per-dataset cost grows with the number of molecules (every fit), with the total width of the pool (importance scoring over every feature) and with the number of representations.
% - Caching repeated work (fold splits, importance scores, per-representation baseline scores) cuts the cost of a re-run. It does not cut the cost of the first run.
% - TODO(results): wall-clock hours per dataset for the full search, and which axis dominates the time. Numbers come from the full runs.
% - DIAGRAM: run time (hours, log scale) against dataset size for the full search. One point per dataset, coloured by domain. A horizontal reference line for the time to fit the best single representation once.
% - Why it matters: the search costs orders of magnitude more than fitting one model on one representation. It needs a cluster, not a laptop, which limits who can use it.
% - Links to: "Fast tier and adaptive pruning" (Methods, this chapter), the partial answer already built. Links to "Future directions" (Chapter 7), where speeding up the search is a next step.
%
% The search must be repeated when inputs change:
% - A search result is fitted to one dataset, one set of base representations, one downstream model and one split.
% - New molecules or different folds: the importance scores and the per-representation scores change, so the split of the budget and the selected features can change.
% - A new base representation added to the pool: every representation's share of the budget is set relative to the others, so the whole search reruns, not just the part for the new representation.
% - A new downstream model: the best configuration is chosen per model, so a new model needs its own search.
% - This is the same kind of cost as re-tuning hyperparameters, but over a larger space.
% - Why it matters: datasets grow over time (new assay rounds, new chemical series). Each update means paying for the search again.
% - Links to: "Blueprints" (Methods, this chapter). Reusing a recipe on a related dataset is the partial workaround; its limits belong here.
% - TODO(results): how far a recipe carries to a new split or a related dataset (overlap of selected features, change in score). Numbers come from the full runs.
%
% High upfront cost, unknown gain:
% - The gain is only known after the search has run.
% - Some datasets are not helped, typically when one representation dominates (Discussion, "Where the hybrid does not help"); point there.
% - No cheap test run before the search predicts whether the hybrid will beat the best single representation.
% - Why it matters: on some datasets a user pays the full cost to learn there is no gain. That result still says the single representation is enough, but it is expensive to learn.
% - DIAGRAM: per dataset, hybrid score minus best single representation score (in the dataset's own metric), against search cost in hours. Per-fold points shown, no error bars.
% - Links to: "Discussion" (this chapter), on where hybrids do not help. Links to "No single method fits every dataset" (Chapter 7).
%
% Reproducibility:
% - A hybrid result depends on many settings: the representation pool and the version of each representation, the fold seed, the importance method, the allocation method, the budget, the internal evaluator, and library versions.
% - Small changes to the training set can change which features are selected. The stability index (Complexity experiments) measures this.
% - Some importance methods are stochastic (neural-network attributions, subsampled tree ensembles). The same seed and the same library versions are needed to rebuild the same hybrid.
% - What the blueprint stores and what a rebuild does: see "Blueprints" (Methods); point there.
% - Pretrained embeddings add model-version dependence. A re-released checkpoint changes the representation.
% - Why it matters: a reader who wants to reproduce a number needs the full recipe, not just "a hybrid of ECFP and descriptors".
% - Links to: Appendix "Data and code availability", and Appendix "The search space and its defaults".
%
% Simpler selection may be enough:
% - Simpler options exist: pick the best single representation by cross-validation; concatenate and apply a filter or an L1 penalty; or rely on a tree ensemble's built-in feature selection. Each costs about one model fit or one short tuning run, not a grid.
% - They may reach much of the hybrid's gain on many datasets at a much lower cost. Any of them tuned by cross-validation also needs nested cross-validation for an honest estimate \citep{Varma2006}, so the cost gap is smaller than it first looks.
% - Practical rule to state: with limited compute or a routine task, start with the best single representation. Run the search when a gain beyond the fold-to-fold spread would matter. Links to: "Simple is often better" (Chapter 7) and "Combining representations: what exists" (introduction of this chapter).
%
% Scope of claims:
% - The search space is finite. A better hybrid may exist outside the grid (other budgets, other importance methods). The search only finds the best configuration within the grid.
% - Why it matters: together these limits say the method is a tool for choosing features when a gain is worth the compute. They do not make it a default for every dataset.
% - Links to: "Limitations" in Chapter 7, which covers the thesis as a whole; keep this section to the hybrid method.
% ---- END NOTES 5.10 ----

We want to be clear about KIRBy's limitations. For small datasets (below about 200 samples), the hybrid search cannot reliably outperform the best single source. We suspect this is because the internal validation set within each fold shrinks to roughly 20 samples, and at that size the evaluator cannot distinguish genuine improvements from noise. Tuning cannot fix this. And at the other extreme, frozen embeddings have a ceiling. No signal can be added beyond what the inputs already contain, and on tasks where the true signal requires end-to-end learning from raw inputs (certain 3D quantum-mechanical properties, for example, where SchNet outperforms any frozen embedding by a wide margin), this ceiling binds. Although the methodology naturally accommodates embeddings from sequence-trained models (ESM-2, ProtBERT, etc.), encoding raw sequences directly as one-hot or $k$-mer features disperses the positional and compositional information that makes sequences useful in the first place. Integrating 20 to 50 foundation models into a single pipeline, each with its own dependencies, GPU requirements, and version constraints, is genuinely difficult engineering. Embeddings need only be computed once per dataset and everything is cached. Yet the initial integration burden is real and scales with the number of models in the pool. Finally, on endpoints with high experimental noise, even a well-constructed hybrid may not improve on simpler baselines. Kramer et al.\ \citep{Kramer2012} documented that experimental uncertainty in heterogeneous public $K_i$ data is approximately 0.54 log units. This sets a natural accuracy ceiling of $R^2 \approx 0.81$ regardless of model sophistication; the budget constraint plausibly guards against overshoot, but it cannot push past the noise floor.

\subsection{Similarity in a hybrid}
% OUTLINE 6.2.1: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.2.1 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% This is the one place the neighbour prediction and the weighted distances are defined. Other sections point here.
%
% Two ways to use a hybrid for similarity:
% - Way 1: build an ordinary hybrid (features chosen to help a downstream model), then compute distances in it.
% - Way 2, the similarity hybrid: the same search, but each candidate is ranked by how well each molecule's activity is predicted from its five nearest neighbours in the hybrid. No downstream model is fitted during the search.
% - Same importance scores, allocation methods, budgets and folds in both. Only the score differs: the neighbour score ranks candidates and also sets each representation's share of the budget, so the internal evaluator is not used.
% - Why it matters: Chapter 4 argued the space defines the neighbours. Way 2 tests whether choosing features for neighbourhood quality fixes that more directly than choosing them for prediction.
% - DIAGRAM: two pipelines with a shared first half (base representations -> importance -> allocation -> hybrid). Top: hybrid -> downstream model -> dataset metric. Bottom: hybrid -> 5 nearest neighbours from other scaffold groups -> distance-weighted mean activity -> Spearman. Caption: same search, different score.
%
% Distances in a hybrid:
% - Each feature is z-scored with training-molecule statistics only. Default distance is Manhattan on the z-scored features: $d_{ij} = \sum_m |z_{im} - z_{jm}|$.
% - Width is weight: every z-scored feature adds one term of similar size, so a representation's influence on the distance is roughly its share of the features. In a hybrid, the allocation doubles as a weighting of the representations inside the distance.
% - TODO(decision): distance and neighbour weighting are still being chosen. Confirm the final choice before writing it as settled.
%
% The similarity score:
% - Within each held-out block, scaffold groups are split into parts. Each molecule's 5 nearest neighbours come from other parts, so from other scaffold groups.
%   - Why: a same-scaffold neighbour is an easy match and inflates the score.
% - Prediction: $\hat{y}_i = \frac{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} y_j / d_{ij}}{\sum_{j\in N_5(i)} 1/d_{ij}}$.
% - Score: Spearman $\rho(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})$ for regression; $2(\mathrm{AUROC} - 0.5)$ for binary classification, so chance is 0 in both.
% - Undefined for multiclass labels and where a held-out block holds one group. In code that removes three datasets: tcga_brca_pam50 and tz_her2_multiclass (more than two classes), and tz_her2_binary (a held-out block can hold one edit-distance shell). The similarity experiments are also skipped on the nucleotide datasets. Antibody and other non-small-molecule datasets are not excluded.
% - Why it matters: the score turns "similar molecules have similar activity" into a number \citep{Cherkasov2014}.
%
% Importance-weighted distance (the \paragraph "Feature importance weighting", her prose):
% - Each feature's term in the distance is multiplied by its importance. Negative importances are set to 0, so a zero-importance feature drops out.
% - CHECK (existing prose): $x' = x \odot \sqrt{w}$ is right for Euclidean distance only. Under Manhattan each feature is multiplied by $w$ itself. General rule: exponent $1/p$ for a distance with exponent $p$. State which distance the prose assumes.
% - Weights are applied after z-scoring; before it, z-scoring divides them back out.
% - TODO(results): importance-weighted against unweighted features for predicting each molecule's activity from its nearest neighbours, per dataset.
% - TODO(results): the similarity hybrid against the ordinary hybrid and the best single representations on the similarity score, per fold; and the similarity hybrid's score as input to an ordinary model.
% ---- END NOTES 6.2.1 ----
\todo[inline, color=yellow!40]{6.2.1: objective=similarity. [DIAGRAM D6.1]}

\paragraph{Feature importance weighting.}

Beyond simply using the feature importance scores for allocation, we investigate whether feature weighting by importance during distance computation improves noise detection. For a feature importance vector $w$, weighted distances are computed by scaling the standardised features: $x' = x \odot w$ under Manhattan distance ($x \odot \sqrt{w}$ under Euclidean), with negative importances set to 0 and $w$ rescaled to average 1.

In addition to evaluating hybrid representation quality for distance-based reasoning, our investigation evolved to explore alternative noise handling strategies. Initial experiments with sample removal methods revealed limitations when applied to Gaussian noise affecting all samples. This motivated a pivot toward sample weighting and label smoothing approaches, which do not require accurate binary classification of noisy versus clean samples but instead leverage continuous noise magnitude estimates.

\subsubsection{Sample removal methods}

Distance-based approaches rely on the assumption that similar molecules have similar properties \citep{Cherkasov2014}. Presumably, noisy data would break this pattern, making it a method for detecting anomalous data. k-Nearest neighbor filtering examines the variance of a sample's neighborhood. For sample $x_i$ with $k$ nearest neighbors $\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)$, we compute the standard deviation of neighbor labels:
$$
\sigma_i = \text{std}(\{y_j : x_j \in \mathcal{N}_k(x_i)\})
$$
Samples with $\sigma_i$ exceeding the 90th percentile are marked as potentially noisy. We test $k \in \{5, 10, 20\}$.

Local Outlier Factor (LOF) identifies anomalous samples by detecting local density estimates between a sample and its neighbors \citep{Breunig2000}. These density estimates are based on the distance to its $k$ nearest neighbors. If a point's density differs from that of its neighbors such that the LOF is substantially greater than 2, it is flagged as an outlier.

We also tested the following noise removal methods:  distance-weighted prediction filtering, neighborhood consensus filtering, AC detection, Mahalanobis outlier detection, cross-validation disagreement, bootstrap ensemble filtering, co-teaching \citep{Han2018}, and DivideMix \citep{li2020}.

\subsubsection{Noise magnitude estimation}

% TODO: [CR 863] carries the author's own '% TODO: get sources' marker. Left in place.
We now examine the core of the noise mitigation research: estimating the magnitude of noise. This enables proportional handling rather than complete removal. We used the following neighbor-based estimators:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Neighbor residual: Estimates noise as deviation from k-NN mean, normalized by global label standard deviation:
    $$\text{score}_i = \frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}|}{\sigma_y}$$
    
    \item Local variance ratio: Measures deviation relative to local label variance, identifying samples that deviate from neighbors more than neighbors deviate from each other:
    $$\text{score}_i = \frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}|}{\text{std}(y_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}) + \epsilon}$$
    
    \item AC-aware: Distinguishes noise from activity cliffs by weighting deviation by neighborhood consistency. High deviation with high neighbor agreement suggests noise while high deviation with neighbor disagreement suggests an AC \citep{Dablander2023}:
    $$\text{score}_i = \frac{|y_i - \bar{y}_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)}|}{\sigma_y} \cdot \frac{1}{1 + \text{std}(y_{\mathcal{N}_k(x_i)})/\sigma_y}$$
\end{itemize}

And we used the following model-based estimators using prediction disagreement:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Cross-validation residual: Uses out-of-fold predictions; high residuals indicate labels inconsistent with the model learned from other samples.
    \item Ensemble disagreement: Trains multiple models and combines prediction variance with mean residual.
\end{itemize}

We used probabilistic methods that directly predict noise magnitude using uncertainty estimates:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Gaussian Process: Provides natural uncertainty estimates via posterior variance, with learned noise level $\sigma^2$ from the kernel \citep{gauche}.
    
    \item Heteroscedastic Neural Network: Predicts both $\mu(x)$ and $\sigma^2(x)$ using $\beta$-NLL loss \citep{Seitzer2022}:
    $$\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{(y - \mu)^2}{\sigma^2} + \log \sigma^2\right) \cdot \sigma^{2\beta}$$
    where $\beta = 0.5$ prevents the variance from hiding the mean errors.
    
    \item Conformal prediction: Uses split conformal prediction to obtain prediction intervals. Wider intervals should indicate higher uncertainty \citep{Shafer2008}.
    
    \item Deep ensemble: Trains $M$ independent networks and uses the variance of means as epistemic uncertainty plus the mean of variances as aleatoric uncertainty \citep{lakshminarayanan2017}.
    
    \item Bayesian Random Forest: Uses tree prediction variance and out-of-bag residuals as uncertainty estimates \citep{taddy2015}.
    
    \item Quantile regression: Fits models for multiple quantiles and uses interval width as noise estimate.
    
    \item NGBoost: Natural gradient boosting that predicts distributional parameters $(\mu, \sigma)$ directly \citep{Duan2020}.
\end{itemize}

NGBoost in particular was selected due to its promising uncertainty values with respect to the correlation between noise and prediction error, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:uq}.  

\subsubsection{Label smoothing and sample weighting}

Given the fact that all labels contain some magnitude of noise, we focused strongly on label smoothing over removal using the following methods:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Proportional: $y_{\text{smooth}} = y + \alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}} \cdot (\text{target} - y)$
    
    \item Threshold: Only correct samples above the $(1-\alpha)$ percentile of scores.
    
    \item Soft threshold: Applies sigmoid-like correction strength:
    $$\text{strength} = \alpha \cdot \sigma\left(5 \cdot (\text{score}_{\text{norm}} - 0.5)\right)$$
    
    \item Confidence: For methods that output raw uncertainty $\sigma$ (e.g., NGBoost):
    $$\text{correction} = \frac{\alpha \cdot \sigma}{1 + \alpha \cdot \sigma}, \quad y_{\text{smooth}} = (1 - \text{correction}) \cdot y + \text{correction} \cdot \mu$$
\end{itemize}


Rather than directly modifying labels, we weighted more trustworthy samples during training:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Inverse: $w = \frac{1}{1 + \alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}}}$
    
    \item Exponential: $w = \exp(-\alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}})$
    
    \item Soft: $w = 1 - \alpha \cdot \text{score}_{\text{norm}}^2$, clipped to $[0.1, 1.0]$
\end{itemize}

All weights are normalized to mean 1 to preserve effective sample size.

\subsubsection{Recovery rate}

For noise detection accuracy, we compute precision, recall, and F1 score, treating noise injection as ground truth.

We have a standard RF on ECFP4 train and test on the clean dataset, producing $R^2_{\text{clean}}$. Noise is then added to the clean data, RF on ECFP4 is then freshly trained and tested on this noisy data, producing $R^2_{\text{noisy}}$. Using the given estimation method and representation to test, noise estimation and label smoothing is applied to the noisy data, resulting in the denoised set. RF on ECFP4 is yet again run, this time on the denoised data, to produce $R^2_{\text{denoised}}$. From these performance values, we derive recovery rate, or the fraction of performance loss recovered after denoising, as follows: 

$$
\text{Recovery} = \frac{R^2_{\text{denoised}} - R^2_{\text{noisy}}}{R^2_{\text{clean}} - R^2_{\text{noisy}}}
$$

A recovery rate of 1.0 indicates complete restoration to clean performance; negative values indicate the denoising procedure made performance worse than the noisy baseline.

\subsection{By noise type}
% OUTLINE 6.5.5: bullets in THESIS_OUTLINE.md.
% ---- NOTES 6.5.5 (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope: conceptual discussion. Results sit in the subsections before this one. Noise conditions are defined in Chapter 2; recap each in one line only.
% - This is the one place for "why Gaussian noise is the hardest" and for the noise-type diagram. Other sections point here.
% - DIAGRAM: 2x2 panels (Gaussian, outliers, grouped offsets, censoring). x = true label, y = observed label, diagonal = no noise. Shade what can be detected; mark what can be corrected.
%
% Why Gaussian noise is the hardest:
% - Every label carries error of unknown size and sign. No clean subset exists to learn from.
% - A detector that compares a molecule with its neighbours compares it with labels that are also noisy. A large disagreement can be noise or a real activity cliff.
% - Even a correctly flagged label has no known correction. Removing it loses a molecule; replacing it with a neighbour estimate swaps its noise for the neighbours' noise.
% - The paradox: noise can be identified or estimated but not removed. A cleaned dataset can give a worse model than the noisy one (negative recovery rate; Chapter 4 exploratory study).
% - A better similarity does not solve this. It can make neighbours more alike in true activity; it cannot remove the noise on their labels.
%
% Other noise types:
% - Outliers are easier to find: a few labels stick out against clean or lightly perturbed neighbours.
% - The fix for an outlier is removal, which does not recover the true value.
% - Grouped offsets move a whole group together, so neighbours from the same group share the error and cannot reveal it.
% - Knowing each record's source (laboratory, document) is what makes grouped offsets estimable.
% - Censoring is the most fixable: the location (the limit) and the direction are known; only the size is unknown.
%
% Identify versus correct: the bullets above can be set as a table (noise type; how easy to identify; whether the direction of the error is known).
%
% Practical takeaways (her list):
% - First ask what kind of noise is likely, not only how much.
% - Data pooled from many laboratories or documents: expect grouped offsets; keep the source ids.
% - A single controlled experiment: expect censoring; keep the "<" and ">" qualifiers instead of dropping those records.
% - Molecules from very different chemical domains: neighbours may not be close, so neighbour-based detection is least reliable.
% - Source and amount unknown: training methods that tolerate noise are safer than editing labels (recommendation argued in Chapter 7, Limitations).
% - TODO(results): per noise type, whether any mitigation improved on leaving the labels, on each feature space.
% ---- END NOTES 6.5.5 ----

\section{Limitations}

% Noise mitigation without ground truth:
% - The thesis set out to mitigate label noise. The author's conclusion: it is a hard problem with no clear general solution.
% - Every experimental label carries unknown noise, and there is no clean copy to check a correction against.
% - Identifying a noisy label is easier than correcting it: argued in Chapter 6, "By noise type"; point there.
% - What is possible: outlier detection, and detection of systematic errors with known structure (a shared shift per source; clipping at a limit).
% - In the assay experiments, "clean" means "no added noise", not "true". Only QM9 is close to noise-free.
% - Injected conditions are models of noise; real data mix them in unknown amounts, so a method that works under one condition may fail on real data.
% - TODO(results): how many mitigation methods improved on the uncorrected model, per noise condition, and where recovery went negative.
%
% Recommendation: learning with noisy labels over direct cleaning:
% - Cleaning estimates each label's error and fixes it into the data before training; a wrong guess stays for every later model.
% - Noise-robust training changes how the model trains and adapts throughout training using all the data \citep{Song2022}.
% - Cleaning is cheaper (done once, reused by any model); state that trade-off.
% - Exceptions where direct correction is justified: known direction (censoring) or a clearly separate subset (gross outliers, offsets tied to a source).

% TODO: prune the parts that ch.4-6 now deliver.
I also plan to extend the hybrid research to additional base representations and datasets. Some alternative representations include additional GNN embeddings, sequential encoders, augmented features such as embedded distances from molecular graphs, and transformed representations (i.e., normalization, binarization of continuous features). Additionally, I want to explore alternative feature importance scoring methods, especially ones that involve different families of models. Specifically, I plan to look at TreeSHAP, KernelSHAP, drop-column importance for noise robustness, gradient-based attribution for NN embeddings, and small-loss selection, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:lnl_motivation}. 

As mentioned earlier, although I do not currently have a full set of results on hybrid, they have been showing promise throughout a rank of QSAR and noise mitigation tasks. I would like to explore the complexity tradeoff between model and representation complexity, as I've seen a trend of complicated models like NNs having stronger predictive performance with smaller hybrids and more simplistic tree-based methods benefiting from larger hybrids. This noise mitigation experiment relied on a very small default hybrid and used RF-based feature importance. I would like to leverage my allocation strategies to adapt hybrid models to datasets \textit{and} models. These strategies can also be modified to optimize for noise robustness in addition to predictive performance. 

Various estimation methods showed promise, meriting further investigation. Additionally, neighbor-based noise mitigation strategies which are not fit for Gaussian noise may perform differently on outlier-based strategies. The dominance of estimators with label smoothing and sample weighting may be challenged for different types of noise. 

What does this mean in practice? A new foundation model appears every few months in molecular, protein, or antibody modeling. A researcher using KIRBy does not need to decide whether to replace their current encoding or retrain their pipeline; they compute the new model's embeddings, add the matrix to the pool, and re-run the search. The search determines whether the new embedding contributes useful features and, if so, how many and which ones. In a field where encoding choice is often determined by familiarity or lab convention rather than by systematic comparison, this is a substantive change. It replaces a single bet with a principled combination, tailored to each task, that updates as the pool grows.

% - The activity-cliff sets of \citet{Dablander2023} (dopamine D2, factor Xa, SARS-CoV-2 main protease), if the OPIG activity-cliff task comes back.
% ---- END NOTES A ----

The datasets used in this study are publicly available. The QM9 dataset is available on Figshare \citep{qm9_dataset} and is also redistributed through the OpenADMET benchmark suite \citep{Ramakrishnan2014, openadmet}. The LogD and Caco-2 datasets are available in the OpenADMET repository \citep{openadmet}. The hERG-Ki dataset was derived from ChEMBL \citep{Zdrazil2023} following the curation protocol described in Section~\ref{sec2}, and can be regenerated from ChEMBL using that protocol.

\chapter{The search space and its defaults}\label{app:searchspace}
% ---- NOTES C (2026-09-28, bullets for writing; comment only) ----
% Scope and status:
% - Purpose of this appendix: the options the hybrid search chose from in the thesis runs, their default values, and what a saved blueprint records. Chapter 5 (Building Adaptive Hybrid Representations) gives the reasons; this appendix is the reference.
% - The outline extract under this heading is out of date. Remove from the text: greedy, dominant-greedy, two-stage and stability-selection allocation; MLP and ridge as downstream models; MLP and SVM as listed internal evaluators. None of these is searched any more.
% - Check against Chapter 5: its methods prose still describes greedy allocation, 13 retained allocation methods, and pruning on the first fold with a top-3 rule. The values below are the current ones; the two places should agree before submission.
% - CHECK: Chapter 5 Methods (the search space; fast tier and adaptive pruning) also carries the allocation formulas and the pruning rule. Keep the full version in one place and point to it from the other.
% - Why it matters: this appendix is what lets a reader rebuild any hybrid in the thesis. Every choice the search makes is one of the values listed here.
% - Links to: Chapter 5, sections "Methods: the hybrid search" and "Results: blueprints on specific datasets"; Appendix A (datasets and their groups); Appendix D (extended result tables).
% - TABLE C.1: one row per axis. Columns: axis, values in a full search, values in a fast search. The bullets below fill it.
% Notation:
% - As defined in Chapter 5, "From base representations to a hybrid": $K$ base representations, budget $B$, allocation $b_k$, importance $s^{(k)}_j$, selected set $S_k$ = the $b_k$ features of representation $k$ with the highest importance.
% Axis 1, downstream models (the model that is finally trained and scored on the hybrid):
% - Full search: random forest \citep{Breiman2001}, LightGBM \citep{ke2017lightgbm}, a feed-forward neural network (DNN), XGBoost \citep{Chen2016}, support vector machine \citep{Vapnik1995}.
% - Per-dataset exceptions set for run time (code: tests/experiments/dataset_policy.py): XGBoost is not scored on the two nucleotide datasets; SVM is not scored under capped augmentation on Caco-2, LogD, RLM, MDR1 and the Buchwald-Hartwig plates, nor under uncapped on Koenig expression; random forest is not scored under uncapped on LogD and QM9, nor under capped on QM9 (SVM too). The GP is added on humanness. On datasets under 1,000 molecules a Gaussian process is added as a sixth (regression: RBF plus white-noise kernel, 3 optimiser restarts; classification: RBF kernel), except where the dataset's skip table drops it.
% - Fast search: the same five. The fast tier shortens the allocation, importance and representation-count lists only; all five downstream models are kept.
% - The near-unregularised linear model used only in the budget-overfitting experiment: least squares with penalty $\alpha = 10^{-6}$ (minimum-norm fit when $d > n$); logistic regression with $C = 10^{6}$ for classification. Its parameter count equals the budget.
% - Default settings of each downstream model, the DNN architecture, k-nearest neighbours ($k = 5$, importance-weighted distance experiment only) and the SVM size limit: see Chapter 5 "Models and metrics" (NOTES 5.3.3).
% - No model has a cap on budget. Every model is searched at every budget.
% - Hyperparameters are defaults unless tuning is switched on (tuning setup: Chapter 5 "Models and metrics"). When on, tuning runs inside each fold, on the training rows of the matrix the model is fitted on (one representation for a baseline, the selected features for a hybrid).
% Axis 2, budget $B$ (maximum number of features in the hybrid):
% - Values: $B \in \{10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2000\}$.
% - The best result per model is the best over all budgets, so a small budget can win.
% - Budgets larger than the number of training molecules are kept and searched, not dropped.
% - Why it matters: the budget sets the size of the input the model sees, and for the similarity uses it sets how many features enter the distance between molecules. Links to: Chapter 5 results on when the hybrid helps; Chapter 6.
% Axis 3, number of representations $N$:
% - Values: $N \in \{2, 3, 5, K\}$; $K$ = the whole pool (10 on most datasets). Fast search: $\{3, K\}$.
% - For each fold, the $N$ representations with the highest standalone score (see Axis 4) are kept; the rest get $b_k = 0$.
% - The count and the weighting are separate choices, so any weighting can be paired with any count.
% Axis 4, allocation method (how $B$ is split across representations):
% - Step 1, standalone score: each representation alone is scored by the internal evaluator on an internal validation split of the training molecules, giving $s_k$ in the dataset's own metric.
% - Step 2, rescale so 0 means "no better than a trivial model":
%   - correlation metrics (Spearman, Pearson, MCC, $R^2$): $\tilde{s}_k = \max(0, s_k)$
%   - AUROC and concordance index: $\tilde{s}_k = \max(0,\, 2(s_k - 0.5))$
%   - RMSE and MAE: $\tilde{s}_k = \max\!\left(0,\, 1 - \mathrm{err}_k / \mathrm{err}_0\right)$, where $\mathrm{err}_0$ is the error of predicting the training mean for every held-out molecule (RMSE) or the training median (MAE)
%   - weighted F1 or accuracy: $\tilde{s}_k = \max\!\left(0,\, \frac{s_k - s_0}{1 - s_0}\right)$, where $s_0$ is the score of always predicting the majority training class
% - Step 3, weights $w_k$ from $\tilde{s}_k$. The five searched methods:
%   - linear: $w_k = \tilde{s}_k$
%   - squared: $w_k = \tilde{s}_k^{\,2}$
%   - softmax with temperature $T=1$: $w_k = \exp\!\left((\tilde{s}_k - \max_j \tilde{s}_j)/T\right)$
%   - rank with exponential decay: $w_k = \exp(-0.3\, r_k)$, where $r_k = 0$ for the best representation, 1 for the second, and so on
%   - global pool with squared weights (described below)
% - Step 4, the first four methods give each representation a fixed number of features: $b_k = \frac{w_k}{\sum_j w_j} B$, capped at $d_k$.
%   - Rounding uses the largest-remainder rule: floor every $b_k$, then give the leftover features one at a time in order of the largest fractional part.
%   - A representation with $w_k > 0$ keeps at least one feature.
%   - If every weight is zero, the budget is split equally and a warning is recorded.
% - Global pool method:
%   - Candidate slots per representation: $c_k = \operatorname{round}\!\left(2B \cdot \frac{w_k}{\sum_j w_j}\right)$ with $w_k = \tilde{s}_k^{\,2}$, capped at $d_k$.
%   - Take the top $c_k$ features of each representation by importance, among those with importance above 0, and join them into one pool of at most $2B$ features.
%   - Re-rank the whole pool with one importance fit and keep at most the top $B$, dropping any feature that fit scores at exactly 0.
%   - So $b_k$ is set by competition inside the pool, not fixed ahead of time.
% - Fast search: linear, squared, rank with exponential decay.
% Axis 5, importance method (how $s^{(k)}_j$ is computed):
% - Always fitted separately per representation, on training molecules only. Features are standardised first.
% - LightGBM gain: total loss reduction from splits on feature $j$; 50 trees, depth 6 \citep{ke2017lightgbm}.
% - TreeSHAP: mean absolute SHAP value over molecules, from a 50-tree random forest of depth 10 \citep{Lundberg2020}.
% - DeepLIFT: mean absolute attribution against an all-zero reference input, from a small network (hidden layers 64 and 32, 100 epochs, Adam) \citep{Shrikumar2017}.
%   - Because features are standardised, the all-zero reference is the training mean of every feature.
% - For the two tree methods, a representation with more than 2,000 features is first cut to its 2,000 highest-variance features. Variance is measured before standardising. The rest get importance 0.
% - Fast search: LightGBM gain only.
% - Links to: Chapter 5 "The search space" (her prose on why importance depends on the model).
% Axis 6, internal evaluator (the model that scores each representation on its own inside the search; candidate hybrids are scored by the downstream model):
% - Values: "match" (the same model type as the downstream model), random forest, LightGBM, DNN. The fast search uses the same four.
% - The evaluator models are lighter than the downstream ones: random forest 50 trees, depth 10; LightGBM 50 trees, depth 4; DNN the same as downstream.
% - "match" becomes XGBoost (50 trees, depth 4) or SVM (RBF kernel) for those downstream models. On datasets under 1,000 molecules it also becomes a GP (RBF kernel) for the GP. So five evaluator types appear in a full search, six where the GP is added, although four are listed.
% - Why it matters: tests whether the features a model prefers depend on that model's own inductive bias (tree ensemble, boosted trees, neural network). Links to: Chapter 5 "Why each axis is swept".
% Axis 7, objective (what the search maximises; chosen per run, not swept inside one run):
% - Prediction (default): the downstream model's score in the dataset's metric.
% - Similarity: each held-out molecule's activity predicted from its five nearest neighbours in the hybrid; the score, and where it is undefined, are defined in Chapter 6, "Similarity in a hybrid".
% - Links to: Chapter 4 (why similarity in fixed representations fails); Chapter 6 (Hybrid Representations for Similarity and Label Noise).
% Axis 8, augmentation (a separate run per setting):
% - Purpose: small feature sets (for example MACCS keys) that cannot compete as whole representations get a separate route into the hybrid. Already in prose: the motivation (Chapter 5 "Augmentation").
% - Settings: none; uncapped (extra features appended, total can exceed $B$); capped (extra features replace the base's weakest, total stays $\le B$).
% - Candidate ranking, block sizes and the capped rule: Chapter 5, "Augmentation"; not repeated here. Two details not stated there: the residual split is grouped 2- or 3-fold, and no admission test is applied beyond the block comparison.
% Size of the search (the one place the configuration count is given; Chapter 5 Limitations points here):
% - Configurations per (downstream model, budget), full: $|\text{allocation}| \times |\text{importance}| \times |\text{evaluator}| \times |N| = 5 \times 3 \times |\text{evaluator}| \times 4$. "Matched" is dropped where it repeats an evaluator already listed, so random forest, LightGBM and DNN have 3 evaluators (180 configurations) and XGBoost and SVM have 4 (240).
% - That is 17 (downstream model, internal evaluator) pairs, and $5 \times 3 \times 4 \times 17 \times 8 \text{ budgets} = 8{,}160$ configurations per dataset, objective and augmentation setting (full).
% - Fast: $3 \times 1 \times 2 \times 17 \times 8 = 816$. All five downstream models are kept in the fast runs; only the allocation, importance and representation-count lists are shorter.
% - On datasets under 1,000 molecules the Gaussian process is a sixth downstream model, with its own matched evaluator, which adds to these counts.
% - Every configuration is scored on each of the 5 outer folds, except those removed by adaptive pruning (Chapter 5, "Fast tier and adaptive pruning"), which are scored on the first two folds only.
% - Each configuration's score is stored by its settings, not by the tier, so a later full search reuses what a fast search scored and runs only the rest.
% Splitting inside the search:
% - Outer: the 5 grouped folds of Chapter 5 'Datasets and splitting'.
% - Inner: inside each outer training fold, the first fold of a grouped 5-fold split gives a 20% internal validation set. The standalone scores and the augmentation blocks are measured on it.
% - Median imputation and standardisation use training-fold statistics only. Already in prose (Chapter 5 "From base representations to a hybrid").
% - Links to: Appendix A (groups per dataset).
% Adaptive pruning (off unless switched on): the rule, its cut-offs and the funnel diagram are given once, in Chapter 5, "Fast tier and adaptive pruning"; point there.
% Blueprint fields (what is saved for each winning configuration):
% - The recipe: the list of representations searched; the allocation method; the importance method; the budget; the internal evaluator; the number of representations used $N$; the objective; the metric and whether lower is better; the task type; the number of folds; the random seed.
% - Augmentation settings: strategy (none, uncapped, capped); candidate ranking rule; candidate list length (50); optional cap per source; margin a block must clear over $k=0$; optional fraction of the budget used instead of the block sizes.
% - Data recorded alongside the recipe (not part of the recipe):
%   - each representation's best full-width cross-validated score across the downstream models, over every row the search ran on (sign flipped for lower-is-better metrics such as RMSE, so higher is better). This is not the internal-evaluator score that splits the budget (Axis 4); no rebuild reads it, it is kept as provenance
%   - the number of features each representation got, per fold ($b_k$ per fold)
%   - the extra features kept by augmentation, per fold
% - Rebuilding from a blueprint, and reuse on unlabelled molecules: Chapter 5, "Blueprints"; point there.
% - Why it matters: the blueprint is what makes a hybrid readable. It says which representations and which named features (for example descriptors, substructure keys) were chosen. Links to: Chapter 5 "Blueprints" and "Results: blueprints on specific datasets".
% - TODO(results): per-dataset table of the winning blueprint (model, budget, allocation, importance, evaluator, $N$); numbers come from the full runs.
% - TODO(results): per-axis count of how often each value wins, by dataset (with Table C.1 or in Appendix D); numbers come from the full runs.

%      software artifacts. (1) NoiseInject: the open-source benchmarking framework, MIT, at
%      github.com/adpunt/noise_inject, archived on Zenodo; released. (2) The standalone Rust and
%      Python chemical-processing and precompute pipeline. (3) The KIRBy research codebase: pure
%      Python. KIRBy contains no Rust; it operates on precomputed numpy feature matrices.
% ---- end outline extract ----

The NoiseInject benchmarking framework, implementing all six regression and six classification noise injection strategies together with the robustness metrics (noise degradation slope and retention) and uncertainty-calibration metrics (ECE, coverage at $1\sigma$/$2\sigma$, mean interval width, and uncertainty--error / uncertainty--noise correlation) described in this work, is available as an open-source Python package under an MIT license \citep{noiseinject}. It also contains optional reference wrappers (a Gaussian process, split-conformal prediction, and Monte Carlo dropout) that produce per-sample uncertainty for use with these metrics. NoiseInject is model-agnostic, operates on label arrays (NumPy arrays or pandas Series), and is designed to integrate into existing cheminformatics or other data pipelines. Worked examples covering molecular-descriptor, fingerprint, and GNN-embedding workflows are provided in the repository.

\begin{itemize}
    \item Project name: NoiseInject
    \item Project home page: \url{https://github.com/adpunt/noise_inject}
    \item Archived version: \url{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.20532710}
    \item Operating system(s): Platform independent
    \item Programming language: Python
    \item Other requirements: See repository \texttt{requirements.txt} for the full dependency list
    \item License: MIT
    \item Any restrictions to use by non-academics: None
\end{itemize}
